===== PAGE 911 =====

===== PAGE 912 =====

===== PAGE 913 =====

===== PAGE 914 =====

===== PAGE 915 =====

===== PAGE 916 =====

===== PAGE 917 =====
•
A PON network consists of 3 modules: OLT, ODN, and ONU/ONT. 
•
An OLT is located in the central office (CO), provides WAN interfaces such as GbE, OC-
3/STM-1, and DS-3 in the upstream direction, and provides 1.244 Gbit/s or 2.488 Gbit/s 
optical interfaces for the PON in the downstream direction. An ONU/ONT is located on 
the user side and provides users with application interfaces such as 10/100BaseT, T1/E1, 
and DS-3. In the downstream direction (from the OLT to the ONU/ONT), broadcast 
mode is adopted for data transmission. In the upstream direction (from the ONU/ONT 
to the OLT), TDMA based on statistical multiplexing is adopted. The PON uses a tree 
topology. When service protection and channel protection are required, a protection 
ring can be added to protect certain ONTs. 
•
ONU: Optical Network Unit
•
ONT: Optical Network Terminal
•
ODN: Optical Distribution Network
•
OLT: Optical Line Terminal

===== PAGE 918 =====
•
With the development of fiber-in copper-out, FTTx network planning becomes more 
and more important. It has the following characteristics:
▫
ONU nodes are moved closer to users and connected to a huge number of sites. 
The ultimate goal of FTTx network evolution is FTTH. Currently, multiple 
applications such as FTTC, FTTB, and FTTH will coexist for a long time, increasing 
network complexity and maintenance risks. The increase in the number of users 
poses new requirements on the reliability of devices and networks. 
▫
Concurrent multi-service access. Tri-network integration and concurrent multi-
service access are the first step towards all IP. A bearer network has many types 
of services and has various challenges from the aspects of data planning, traffic 
classification, security, and service QoS. 
▫
Devices are deployed in various environments. The user-side environments are 
unknown, and device deployment positions need to be properly planned. 
▫
Good design provides accurate data for subsequent engineering construction, 
offline deployment, and service delivery, improving the efficiency of the entire 
process. 
•
Based on the preceding characteristics, comprehensive, forward-looking, and fine-
grained network planning and design in the early stage of FTTx network deployment 
can help improve the network stability, scalability, and maintainability. Therefore, FTTx 
network planning and design become more and more necessary and important. 

===== PAGE 919 =====
•
The FTTx network design focuses on xPON NEs, designs the FTTx topology, logical 
data, service implementation, and interconnection with peripheral subsystems. The 
purpose is to facilitate network deployment, maximize the optimization of network 
indicators, and facilitate subsequent operation and maintenance. Network design is a 
complex and high-end service that involves various application scenarios, device 
selection, network engineering, and O&M.
•
Network design personnel must have theoretical knowledge, product knowledge, 
engineering knowledge, and O&M knowledge or experience.
•
A well-designed FTTx network should have the following features:
▫
For individual users, flexible service mapping and other VLAN planning facilitate 
the access of multiple services. 
▫
For commercial users, hierarchical QoS, high reliability, and high security must be 
provided to ensure user benefits. 
▫
For mobile base station services, native TDM, CESoP for 2G/3G backhaul, and 
high-precision clock synchronization can be implemented.

===== PAGE 920 =====
•
In general, FTTx network design is categorized into the following phases: plan, 
information collection, HLD, LLD, and network deployment. Each phase requires 
related work records or outputs in addition to inputs. For example, in the HLD phase, 
some contract information and device list may be required as design inputs. After the 
design is complete, the HLD report must be provided as an output. The HLD report can 
also be used as the input of the next phase. That is, the LLD of the next phase is based 
on the HLD. 
•
Ensure that each phase has its inputs (documents or information), avoiding mistakes in 
the direction of subsequent work. 
•
LLD: Low Level Design
•
HLD: High Level Design
•
DD: Deployment Design

===== PAGE 921 =====
•
The following describes the main tasks of each part among the 4 key parts of FTTx
network design: 
▫
In the plan formulation and information collection phases, the main tasks are to 
formulate the project plan, including the project team establishment, 
responsibility division, and work plan formulation, and project milestone setting, 
facilitating process monitoring. Collect project-based live-network information, 
including the topology, user quantity, service, resource usage, and configuration. 
Understand customer requirements in detail, such as the services and number of 
users on the target network. 
▫
In the HLD phase, the main tasks focus on the overall design of the network 
architecture, such as the topology, interconnection, and capacity of the new 
network, and the schemes for network reliability and scalability, and site 
selection. The details are as follows: overall network architecture design, network 
capacity and reliability design, and scalability design coverage and selection. 
▫
In the LLD design phase, the main tasks focus on detailed network design and 
data planning, for example, how to plan VLANs and IP addresses, how to name 
devices, how to implement reliability and QoS, and how to access services. If site 
selection is involved, site design and planning need to be considered. Specify the 
configuration plan and interconnection parameters. The detailed steps include 
the following: VLAN design, IP design, TCONT/GEM port design, system naming 
and numbering design, reliability design, QoS planning, security planning, O&M 
and service provisioning design, and service access design. If necessary, you can 
also plan the selection of OLT and ONU sites. 

===== PAGE 922 =====
▫
In the deployment design phase, the main task is to prepare the configuration 
scripts of each site for deployment. 
•
HLD and LLD are the core and most important tasks during the entire design process. 
The HLD report of a Huawei FTTx network is usually in PPT format, which facilitates 
communication with customers. The LLD report is usually a Word document, which 
helps users to read the LLD report during network construction. 

===== PAGE 923 =====

===== PAGE 924 =====
•
During FTTx network HLD, perform the overall design of the target network 
architecture first. That is, the topology relationship of the target network must be 
determined first, including the connections between NEs on the target network, and 
between NEs on the target network and other NEs, NE location, NE capacity, service 
carrying mode, network evolution requirements, and solutions. 
•
Before designing a topology, you must understand customers' requirements on the 
network topology, networking scale, and reliability. 
•
Comply with the following principles during network topology design:
▫
Ensure that the network has high scalability, maintainability, and feasibility, and 
avoid a complex network structure. 
▫
Ensure that the network has high security and reliability to meet existing or 
future service requirements. 
▫
Use live-network resources whenever possible to protect customers' investment. 
▫
Ensure smooth network evolution and satisfy the service development 
requirements in the appropriate period.  

===== PAGE 925 =====
•
OLTs on a PON network mainly perform functions such as service port aggregation 
and traffic aggregation. The functions and supported user quantity of OLTs are similar 
to those of aggregation switches. Based on the network flattening evolution 
requirements and OLT development trend, OLTs will completely replace aggregation 
switches after the PON network is deployed in a larger scale, forming a large-capacity 
access network in which OLTs are the main devices and fewer COs are required. The 
access network adopts the three-level structure: BAS/SR (MAN edge) - OLT - ONU. 
When deploying an OLT, comply with the following principles: 
•
Generally, an OLT is connected to the BAS through the aggregation switch and 
supports only one-level aggregation. Currently, the constraints for the direct 
connection between an OLT and a BAS/SR are as follows: 
▫
1. In the early stage of service development, the upstream traffic of the OLT is 
less than 1 Gbit/s. The optical cable resources are insufficient, and no optical 
cable between the OLT and the BAS is available. 
▫
2. BAS ports are insufficient and need to be extended by an aggregation switch. If 
the transmission distance is excessivelylong, the optical fiber cannot meet the 
requirement of directly connecting the OLT to the BAS.  
•
You are advised to differentiate the OLT deployment locations. In urban areas, limit 
the coverage of an OLT to 5–10 km and deploy the OLT in a telecommunications room 
generally. In rural areas, deploy an OLT in the office of a town/village based on actual 
network conditions. In case of a small split ratio (less than or equal to 1:8), the 
coverage of an OLT can reach 20 km. 

===== PAGE 926 =====
•
On an OLT network, the upstream traffic needs to be considered. When an OLT 
aggregates services of multiple users, the maximum upstream traffic exceeds 1 Gbit/s, 
and the conditions (BAS port, optical fiber resources, and transmission distance) are 
met, connect the OLT to the BAS in the upstream direction. When the maximum traffic 
of the upstream port on an OLT is less than 1 Gbit/s, connect the OLT to a BAS in the 
upstream direction using an aggregation switch. To minimize the risk of service 
interruption, configure 2 control boards and 2 upstream boards on the OLT, and 
periodically detect the upstream traffic changes of the OLT. If the upstream traffic is 
stable at 1 Gbit/s or above, consider to directly connect the OLT to a BAS. 
•
On an OLT network, the number of users needs to be considered. Due to the limited 
processing capabilities of OLTs, you are advised to limit the maximum number of users 
who have recently accessed each OLT cannot exceed 10,000. 
•
Simplify network construction, configuration, maintenance, and management 
whenever possible, and improve network scalability. Try to adopt a unified networking 
mode and a unified service configuration profile for application scenarios such as FTTH 
and FTTB to simplify network construction, maintenance, and management, and 
improve network scalability. An OLT can be applied to the scenario where both FTTB 
and FTTH are provided, but the PON ports used in FTTH and FTTB networks must be 
separated. That is, a PON port can be used for only one type of service access, FTTH 
access, FTTB (LAN) access, or FTTB (DSL) access. Government and enterprise users 
with high-bandwidth requirements can be connected to the OLT in P2P mode using 
FE/GE ports, ensuring exclusive bandwidth for these users. To ensure reliability, you are 
advised to connect ONUs in key offices to an OLT in type B protection mode, and 
connect OLTs in key offices to upper-layer aggregation devices in link aggregation 
protection mode using upstream ports.

===== PAGE 927 =====
•
For FTTH applications: 
▫
Try to deploy an ONU at the user home, and avoid deploying the ONU at the 
door or in the corridor. For common users, install an ONU in the user terminal 
intelligent box for protection, or place the ONU on the desktop (using the access 
terminal box).  
•
For FTTB applications: 
▫
You can uniformly deploy ONUs in different places, such as corridors in buildings 
or cabinets in telecom risers. To facilitate subsequent maintenance, ensure that 
ONUs are of the same model whenever possible. 
•
For ONUs connected to the same PON port:
▫
Do not plan FTTH and FTTB applications on the same PON port. When both 
MDU and ONT are connected to the same PON port, the upstream bandwidth 
cannot be fairly scheduled. As a result, all users connected to the MDU have the 
same priority and bandwidth as the user connected to the ONT. In this situation, 
users connected to the MDU may not be promptly and fairly scheduled. Guide 
customers to plan FTTH and FTTB applications on separate PON ports in 
separate areas. 

===== PAGE 928 =====
•
Note:
▫
In an FTTB/FTTC solution, the split ratio is selected only based on service 
bandwidth requirements. The transmission distance is not a key factor in the 
FTTB solution because the typical radius of an MDU's coverage is about 1.5 km 
and optical fibers are already routed to a building. The actual access rate 
depends on whether ADSL2+ or VDSL2 is used. 
▫
The ODN of an FTTB/FTTC network commonly uses a split ratio of 1:2, 1:8, or 
1:16. The specific split ratio used depends on the service bandwidth convergence 
ratio on the MDU and the planned service bandwidth for each user. The 
following formula shows how to calculate the split ratio:
▫
Split ratio = PON downstream rate/(Bandwidth per user  x Number of users x 
Convergence ratio on the MDU side)
▫
Convergence ratio on the MDU side = Concurrency ratio on the MDU x Service 
online ratio on the MDU

===== PAGE 929 =====
•
This networking diagram is an example of the overall network architecture design. 

===== PAGE 930 =====

===== PAGE 931 =====
•
Pay attention to the following points during traffic planning and design:
▫
Specify the service type, analyze the service traffic, and calculate the number of 
users covered by PON ports. Determine the type and development scale of the 
services to be carried, and then analyze the service traffic to calculate the 
number of users covered by PON ports. The number of users covered by PON 
ports provides a basis for network topology and capacity design as well as 
network development and capacity expansion. 
▫
The number of users covered by each PON port varies depending on the user 
occupancy rate and service penetration rate. At the initial stage of fiber-in 
copper-out construction, the user occupancy rate and the broadband and IPTV 
penetration rates in new areas and reconstructed areas are probably low, and the 
local networks of different cities may differ a lot. Therefore, when the service 
penetration rate is different, the number of users covered by each PON port is 
different. When the service penetration rate is low, FTTx is mainly used for 
narrowband voice service access. Considering the PON board's capability of 
processing voice services, the number of voice users on each PON port should not 
exceed 1024. The service penetration rate of 70% to 80% can be regarded as a 
high penetration rate. In this case, the number of users on each PON port is 256 
to 512, which can meet recent and relatively long-term requirements. 

===== PAGE 932 =====
▫
In FTTB, focus on the traffic model of the multicast service. Considering the 
channel switching speed of end users, it is recommended that two-level (OLT and 
ONU) multicast duplication be adopted for the multicast service and that 
multicast streams be diverted to the OLT statically. In FTTB, a PON port is 
connected to multiple multicast users. At service peak, all multicast programs 
may be concurrently watched. Therefore, to ensure the multicast service quality, 
multicast bandwidth needs to be pre-allocated to a PON port. Assuming that 
there are 20 high definition (HD) program sources and 80 standard definition 
(SD) program sources, 400 Mbit/s (20 x 8 Mbit/s + 80 x 2 Mbit/s) bandwidth of a 
PON port is fixedly occupied. 
▫
Generally, the recommended available bandwidth on an EPON port is 950 Mbit/s 
and that on a GPON port is 2200 Mbit/s. 

===== PAGE 933 =====
•
Duty cycle: indicates the ratio of the actually used bandwidth to the applied bandwidth. 
For example, the applied bandwidth is 4 Mbit/s and only 500 kbit/s is used.
•
Occupancy rate: indicates the coverage rate. 

===== PAGE 934 =====

===== PAGE 935 =====

===== PAGE 936 =====
•
Site selection design aims to determine the place where the OLT or ONU is located. If 
the location of the OLT or ONU is changed, the deployment of the entire ODN will be 
affected. Once the location of a device is determined, it is difficult or not allowed to 
change. This is because the reconstruction costs of the pipeline resources and the splice 
box are high. 
•
In the HLD phase, site selection design needs to first determine the region division. In 
general, regions are divided based on administrative regions. Then determine the 
device models based on the physical locations of key devices and different scenarios 
and service requirements. 
•
In the LLD phase, plan the detailed design of each site. Before the design, prepare 
related information materials, such as the HLD document, Google map, distribution 
cable drawing of the splice box, and construction principles of the carriers. Provide the 
design based on the construction experience and promotion of carriers on the existing 
transmission network and copper cable network. In addition, refer to the technical 
specifications of carriers and municipal departments for telecom network construction 
and city construction. 
•
During the design, detailed scenario analysis needs to be performed as follows:
•
Analyze the characteristics of regional users and determine the access mode based on 
the user types and service requirements. Survey the service requirements and 
bandwidth models of various types of users to determine the FTTx access mode 
(FTTH/FTTx+LAN/FTTx+DSL) for different types of users. In non-FTTH access mode, 
calculate the number of users covered by each PON port. In DSL access mode, 
determine the maximum distance between an ONU and end users. 
•
Analyze the physical characteristics of the regions and analyze the building 
characteristics (including the number of households, number of buildings, drawings, 
block numbers, building numbers, installation space, and power supply) to determine 
the number and distribution of ONUs, check whether the building characteristics meet

===== PAGE 937 =====
the actual conditions and check the onsite environment. For example, check whether the 
equipment room has sufficient space for equipment and cabling. 
•
Analyze the drop resources (optical fibers, category-5 cables, twisted pair cables, and 
line quality) to determine the ONU type. For example, select type A ONUs when 
optical fibers are used as drop resources, select type B ONUs when category-5 cables 
are used as drop resources, and select type C ONUs when twisted-pair cables are used 
as drop resources. Site survey is required. 
•
Analyze regional network characteristics: Analyze the telecom pipe information and 
the distribution coverage of the splice box (mainly referred to the access of twisted 
pair cables). When selecting the site location, try to make the sites closer to the 
original telecom pipe resources. Site survey is required. 
•
Evaluate the sites, comprehensively balance various factors, and review the site survey 
result to determine the final site location. After the evaluation, determine the OLT 
device solution, such as the OLT location, type, quantity, coverage range, and 
configuration information. In addition, determine the ONU device solution After 
determining the ONU location, you can determine the coverage range of the ONU and 
the current configuration of the ONU based on the existing user service information in 
coverage range, and determine the number of ONUs and the number of required PON 
ports based on the number of households in this range (considering service 
development requirements and potential users in 5 years). 
•
Output the design deliverables. The deliverables mainly include site selection reports 
and survey reports.

===== PAGE 938 =====
•
The OLT deployment position has a great impact on the network structure and layer, 
device management and maintenance, user installation rate, and investment. In 
addition, the OLT location and setting mode must be determined based on the OLT 
coverage capability. In principle, OLTs are deployed in a centralized manner to reduce 
the number of sites and maintenance costs in equipment rooms. 
•
Try to use the existing equipment room to deploy an OLT and do not create an access 
point because of OLT deployment. When determining the specific location of the OLT, 
consider the user distribution to decide whether to deploy the OLT in the CO, module 
exchange, or good AN equipment room (optical aggregation point). In addition, ensure 
that the access optical cables connected to the outside of the office are sufficient so 
that optical cables to each AN equipment room in the CO are available. 
•
Follow the principle of "large capacity and fewer offices". In urban areas, deploying an 
OLT in the convergence equipment room is an optimal choice, which features low 
investment and low OM cost. 
•
In a residential community or area with a high user density, move an OLT closer to 
users based on actual conditions, that is, deploy the OLT in the AN equipment room. 
This saves backbone optical cables. 
•
Determine the location of an OLT based on the coverage scope of the OLT. On a 
typical city PON network, the ODN between the OLT and ONUs has about 7 movable 
connectors. If the PON network adopts the 1:32 split ratio (that is, FTTH), the 
transmission distance of the OLT can reach 5 km. If the PON network adopts the 1:16 
split ratio (that is, FTTB), the transmission distance of the OLT can reach 10 km. If the 
number of movable connectors is reduced and the split ratio is 1:8, the transmission 
distance of the OLT can reach 20 km. In conclusion, the attenuation between the OLT 
and the ONUs complies with the sensitivity range of the OLT. 
•
Based on the theoretical model calculation, the comprehensive cost is optimal when 
the coverage radius of an OLT is 5 km. 

===== PAGE 939 =====
•
An ONU can be installed in a cabinet or network box in indoor and outdoor 
environments. At the initial stage of ONU site selection, survey the site whenever 
possible, determine whether the previous analysis is consistent with the actual 
situation, and determine 1 or 2 suitable sites.
•
For an area that adopts FTTx+DSL access, you are advised to consider both the 
distribution coverage of the FDT and the building characteristics (including the number 
of households, number of buildings, installation space, and power supply) to determine 
the location of the ONUs. 
•
For the area that adopts LAN access, you are advised to consider only the building 
characteristics and determine the ONU location based on the household distribution. 
•
The ONU connected to the DSL port on the OLT should be within 500 m from the user. 
In FTTN, the ONU should be within 800 m from the user. Generally, an ONU is 
configured in each independent unit in a building. If the building has a few floors, the 
distance between the units is near, and the bottom layer is connected, 2 or 3 units can 
share the same ONU. For an ONU connected to the DSL port on the OLT, try to deploy 
it in spacious locations, such as the underground parking lot and staircase. The 
dimensions of the space where the ONU is installed cannot be smaller than 1500 mm 
x 700 mm x 600 mm (height x width x depth). The specific dimensions depend on the 
cabinet dimensions. 
•
The asterisk (*) behind the table indicates the preferred priority for site installation. *A 
site with more asterisks has a higher priority.

===== PAGE 940 =====

===== PAGE 941 =====
•
VLAN design is an important part of FTTx network design. Before the design, prepare 
some information or materials as the design inputs, mainly the network topology, 
network mode (FTTH or FTTB), PON port user coverage, and BRAS specifications. 
During the design, consider the requirements for high scalability, maintainability, 
feasibility, and security. In addition, consider smooth network evolution requirements 
and future service application requirements. 
•
The design should cover VLAN allocation scenarios, and S-VLAN, management VLAN, 
and MVLAN allocation principles. The LLD lists the allocation number and range of 
each S-VLAN, management VLAN, and MVLAN.

===== PAGE 942 =====
•
Determine the VLAN requirements for different types of users and services based on 
the services and user types that need to be considered. For example, determine 
whether exact binding or multicast service planning is required. 
•
Determine which services require the S-VLAN+C-VLAN planning (QinQ or stacking 
VLAN) based on the OLT network topology and user quantity on the OLT. If certain 
services require double VLAN tags, determine the inner VLAN (generally called C-
VLAN), outer VLAN (usually called S-VLAN) and the range of the inner VLAN. 
Determine the VLAN range of each S-VLAN. 
•
In GPON access mode, you can plan the GEM ports and T-CONTs at the same time.

===== PAGE 943 =====
•
During VLAN planning, you need to consider many factors, including services, users, 
and networking. The VLAN planning varies in different situations. In actual applications, 
VLANs can be used to differentiate users or services, or differentiate both users and 
services. 
▫
User classification defined by carriers determine whether services need to be 
differentiated based on VLANs. For broadband private-line users (such as users in 
enterprises and Internet cafes), only Internet access services are available. 
Therefore, VLANs can be used to differentiate private-line users rather than 
services. For common users, VLANs are used to differentiate services. Whether 
the services used by common users require precise binding determine whether 
VLANs need to identify both services and users. That is, the requirements for 
precise binding determine whether per service per user per VLAN (PSPUPV) is 
required. In general, for services without the requirements for precise binding, per 
service per VLAN (PSPV) is required. For services with the requirements for 
precise binding, PSPUPV is required. 
▫
The number of users connected to the OLT can determine whether the VLAN is a 
single-tagged or double-tagged VLAN. A single-tagged VLAN can be used when 
there are a small number of users. A double-tagged VLAN must be used when 
services need to be differentiated and the VLANs required by users are greater 
than 4K. 

===== PAGE 944 =====
•
The OLT network topology is also one of the key factors for VLAN design. 
▫
Check whether the OLT is connected to the BRAS and SR using an aggregation 
switch. Based on the check result, determine whether QinQ VLAN configuration 
needs to be performed on the OLT and which device a double-tagged VLAN 
needs to be added. When the OLT aggregates the upstream traffic using an 
aggregation switch, the OLT functions as a campus switch and QinQ VLAN 
configuration is not required (only a single-tagged VLAN is required on the OLT. 
Instead, QinQ VLAN configuration is implemented on the aggregation switch. 
Therefore, the number of users connected to the OLT in such a network topology 
cannot exceed 4K. 
▫
When the number of access users increases, you are advised to implement QinQ 
configuration on the OLT and enable the aggregation switch to transparently 
transmit only VLANs of the OLT. In this scenario, the VLAN planning on the OLT 
is similar to that when the OLT connects directly to the BRAS and SR. However, 
because the aggregation switch is connected to multiple OLTs, the range of outer 
VLAN IDs on the OLT is limited. Then, check whether the network topology can 
be changed and whether the OLT is directly connected to the BRAS or SR. 
•
Whether the OLT upstream metro networking mode is a single-edge (MSE) or multi-
edge (SR+BRAS) is also one key factor that needs to be considered during VLAN design. 

===== PAGE 945 =====
•
Currently in China, the mainstream VLAN planning solutions for an FTTx network are
as follows: For security purposes, precise binding of S-VLAN+C-VLAN is used for HSI 
services on an OLT to differentiate users. For other services such as VoIP and IPTV, 
single-tagged VLANs are used to differentiate users. For outer VLAN in QinQ, 
preferentially allocate S-VLANs by slot on an OLT, or allocate S-VLANs by OLT.  The 
former is recommended. 

===== PAGE 946 =====
•
When HSI services use double-tagged VLANs to precisely bind users, the following 
solutions are available: user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN; user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> 
S+CVALN; user VLAN -> S+C-VLAN > S+C-VLAN. The solutions describe the sequence 
of adding or switching VLAN tags in data flows during the Internet access process and 
the devices that complete VLAN switching or adding. 
•
Solution 1: user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN. The upstream data of an HSI user is 
switched from user VLAN to C-VLAN on the ONU. In this case, the user data is tagged 
with C-VLAN and sent to an OLT. The OLT completes the switching from C-VLAN to S-
VLAN+C-VLAN. Different C-VLANs can be used to identify each user under a PON port. 
The S-VLAN (stacking attribute) identifies the PON port and service. The VLAN 
switching solution is recommended for EPON and GPON. 
•
Solution 2: user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN. The upstream data of an HSI user is 
switched from user VLAN to C-VLAN on the ONU. Each C-VLAN identifies a user under 
a PON port. The user data is tagged with C-VLAN and sent to an OLT. On the OLT, an 
S-VLAN (stacking attribute) is tagged to identify the PON port and service. 
•
Solution 3: user VLAN -> S+C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN. The upstream data of an HSI user is 
switched from user VLAN to C-VLAN on the ONU, and then S-VLANs are added for the 
data. Each MDU is allocated with an S-VLAN. The S-VLAN of a PON port connected to 
an MDU is allocated to the MDU. Each C-VLAN identifies a user connected to a PON 
port. User data is transmitted to the OLT using a double-tagged VLAN with S+C-VLANs. 
The OLT only transparently transmits user data. That is, all double-tagged VLANs in 
this solution are added on ONUs. This solution is not recommended on an EPON 
network. On a GPON network, if the number of GEM port mappings configured on an 
ONU exceeds 128, only this solution can be used.

===== PAGE 947 =====
•
This example provides a detailed plan for the 3 VLAN switching solutions. 
•
In solution 1 (user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN), C-VLANs are planned on ONUs. 
Each port on an ONU is allocated with a C-VLAN. Taking a PON port to which a 24-
port ONU connects as an example, VLAN IDs 1001–1024 are planned. The VLAN IDs 
are uniformly allocated for network-wide ONUs. That is, the VLAN IDs for different 
ONUs can be the same. On an OLT, C-VLANs are uniformly allocated for all PON ports. 
Taking a PON port to which sixteen 24-port ONUs connect as an example, VLAN IDs 
are allocated as follows: VLANs 1001–1024 for ONU1, VLANs 1025–1048 for ONU2, 
and VLANs 1361–1384 for ONU16. The rest can be concluded. On an OLT, S-VLANs are 
uniformly allocated for all PON ports. For example, if the entire OLT functions as a 
GPBC board and has 64 PON ports, the planned VLAN IDs range from 100 to 163. In 
this scenario, S-VLAN 100 is allocated for PON port 0/0/0 on an OLT, 101 is for PON 
port 0/0/1, and 163 is for PON port 0/18/3. The rest can be concluded. 
•
In solution 2 (user VLAN -> C-VLAN -> S+C-VLAN), C-VLANs are uniformly allocated 
for all PON ports on an ONU. Taking a PON port to which sixteen 24-port ONUs 
connect as an example, VLAN IDs are allocated as follows: VLANs 1001–1024 for 
ONU1, VLANs 1025–1048 for ONU2, and VLANs 1361–1384 for ONU16. The rest can 
be concluded. Only S-VLANs 1361–1384 are planned on the OLT. The S-VLAN planning 
method is the same as that in solution 1. On an OLT, S-VLANs are uniformly allocated 
for all PON ports. For example, if the entire OLT functions as a GPBC board and has 64 
PON ports, the planned VLAN IDs range from 100 to 163. In this scenario, S-VLAN 100 
is allocated for PON port 0/0/0 on an OLT, 101 is for PON port 0/0/1, and 163 is for 
PON port 0/18/3. The rest can be concluded. 
•
In solution 3, the C-VLAN planning is the same as that in solution 2. The only 
difference is that S-VLAN configuration is performed on MDUs rather than OLTs. 

===== PAGE 948 =====
•
For non-HSI services, single-tagged VLANs are used. For example, for some unreliable 
VoIP, VOD, and WLAN services, S-VLANs can be added to ONUs, and OLTs are used 
only for transparent transmission. An S-VLAN can be allocated to each service in each 
slot of an OLT. Similar to other VoIP services, for the reliable VoIP services on the ONU 
NMS or MDUs, an S-VLAN can be allocated to each service in each slot of an OLT, and 
related flags are required on the MDUs and OLT. 
•
For the multicast service, VLANs can be planned based on the multicast program 
source. 
•
For the private line service, the mapping from user VLAN to S-VLAN is completed on 
an ONU. For VIP users, an S-VLAN is allocated to each user. For common users, an S-
VLAN is allocated for each service in each slot of an OLT. 
•
For the enterprise L2VPN services, QinQ is directly performed on an ONU, and an outer 
VLAN is an S-VLAN for each service in each slot of the OLT.Only QinQ transparent 
transmission is performed on the OLT. 
•
Note:
▫
Like the HSI service, the VOD service can use S-VLAN+C-VLAN for precise binding. 
Such an application is not described as a new solution. 
▫
For the PON NMS service, each OLT can be allocated with an S-VLAN. For other 
services, it is recommended that each slot of an OLT be allocated with an S-
VLAN. 

===== PAGE 949 =====
•
This example shows how to plan VLANs for multiple services. You can analyze the 
VLAN allocation of the ONU and OLT. 

===== PAGE 950 =====

===== PAGE 951 =====
•
Before IP address planning, analyze the service type and the networking of the upper-
layer bearer network. During IP address planning, consider future requirements for 
scalability, maintainability, feasibility, security, and smooth network evolution. 
•
IP addresses can be classified into service IP addresses and management IP addresses. 
Service IP addresses can be classified into IP addresses for the HSI, multicast, and VoIP 
services. 

===== PAGE 952 =====
•
The principles of IP address design are as follows:
▫
Make full use of the private network IP addresses, save the public network IP 
addresses, and allocate IP address segments based on the service type. 
▫
Broadband Internet access service: obtains a public IP address dynamically using 
PPPoE. 
▫
IPTV service: The terminal STB is connected in IPoE or PPPoE mode and is 
allocated with a private IP address. To obtain a private IP address dynamically 
using PPPoE, a private IP address pool dedicated for the IPTV service needs to be 
allocated on the BRAS. If the OLT supports up to 10,000 IPTV users, among which 
the OLT supports 40% concurrent users, 16 class-C network segments need to be 
allocated. 
▫
VoIP service: The terminal IAD is connected in IPoE mode. With the trend of 
fiber-in copper-out, the VoIP services adopt multiple devices such as AG, IAD, 
ONU with the built-in voice device, home gateway, and SIP software/hardware 
terminal, and therefore a large number of IP addresses are required. The IP 
addresses can be allocated as follows: 
▪
For the IAD embedded in the AG and the FTTB ONU, the private IP address 
for the softswitch is configured statically, and such an IP address can be 
selected from the IP addresses allocated by the carrier.  
▪
For the IAD embedded in the FTTH ONU and the home gateway, the 
private IP addresses are dynamically allocated using DHCP. 

===== PAGE 953 =====
▫
Enterprise users with an intranet: 
▪
For the medium- and large-sized governments and enterprises with an 
intranet, fixed public IP addresses are provided. Except the POTS ports 
provided by the built-in AG of the ONU, the IP addresses of other services 
are provided by the enterprise router and internal data network, and then 
the enterprise egress router performs the NAT translation. 
▫
Enterprise users without an intranet:
▪
For the small-sized governments and enterprises without an intranet, the 
high-speed Internet access service adopts the IPoE mode, and the BRAS 
functions as a DHCP server to allocate public IP addresses.

===== PAGE 954 =====

===== PAGE 955 =====

===== PAGE 956 =====
•
Principles of GEM Port Design
•
GEM port IDs on the same PON port need to be planned in a unified manner. GEM 
port IDs on different PON ports can be the same. 
▫
In FTTH, each service stream of each user (ONU) is allocated with one GEM port 
ID. For example, if each ONU is configured with 8 service streams, the GEM port 
IDs allocated to the ONU can be 0–7. 
▫
In FTTB, for common users, it is recommended that the GEM port be allocated to 
each ONU based on the outer VLAN of the service stream. Note that each GEM 
port corresponds to a maximum of 8 VLANs. 
▫
In FTTB, for VIP broadband private-line users, it is recommended that each 
broadband private-line user be allocated with one GEM port. 
•
For the planning of the mapping from service streams to GEM ports, to simplify the 
operation, it is recommended that the mapping from VLANs to GEM ports be adopted.  

===== PAGE 957 =====
•
The 3 application scenarios in this part are described as follows: 
•
In scenario 1 (FTTB), the user services mainly include the Internet access service, VoIP 
service, and ONU management stream. 
▫
During GEM port planning, all the VoIP packets (one VLAN) can be mapped to 
one GEM port, and the management packets of the ONU (one VLAN) are 
mapped to one GEM port. For all Internet access users, the packets (one VLAN 
for each user) of each high-speed Internet access user are mapped to different 
GEM ports. Therefore, the number of GEM ports on the ONU is 2+N (N indicates 
the number of high-speed Internet access users). 
▫
During T-CONT planning, you can plan 3 T-CONTs for each ONU. Each T-CONT 
corresponds to 3 service streams. For example, T-CONT 1 corresponds to VoIP 
data stream (built-in AG/IAD) and the T-CONT type is set to type 3. 
▫
T-CONT 2 corresponds to device management packets, and the T-CONT type is 
set to type 3. T-CONT 3 corresponds to high-speed Internet access packets, and 
the T-CONT type is set to type 4. 
•
In scenario 2 (FTTO), the user services mainly include the Internet access service, video 
phone service, VoIP service, and ONU management stream. 

===== PAGE 958 =====

===== PAGE 959 =====
•
If a device or port is named or described in a standard manner, it is helpful for 
engineering and maintenance. This helps to quickly identify network nodes or services. 
A device name must clearly show the network level, classification, installation node, 
and type of the device. A port command clearly shows information such as devices and 
ports connected to a link's two ends. You can even describe the VLAN and template in 
detail. 
•
During naming design, carriers have their own device naming rules, follow carriers' 
rules; otherwise, design the naming rules. Comply with the principles of easy expansion, 
easy maintenance, and implementation. Plan the rules on the entire network in a 
unified manner to ensure that the data configurations of NEs of the same type are the 
same. 

===== PAGE 960 =====
•
If carriers have their own device naming rules, follow carriers' rules; otherwise, you are 
advised to follow the following reference naming rules:
▫
Each device name must be unique on the entire network. Therefore, try to 
include the name of the city or office where the device is located in the device 
name. 
▫
A device name can indicate the type of the device, including the device model 
and vendor name. 
▫
A device name can indicate the physical location (location of the equipment 
room) of the device and the detailed site (the community or building). 
▫
For the network devices with the same physical location, they need to have 
different SNs in their names. If multiple devices of the same type exist at the 
same site, use the serial number to distinguish them. 
▫
A device name can indicate the application scenario of the device, or identify the 
application scenario based on the carriers' requirements. 
•
Reference format: area name+office name+device type+application scenario+SN
▫
Area name, office name: identifies the physical location of the device. The 
acronym of the area name/office name is used. 
▫
Device type: identifies the type of the device, for example, MA5600T, MA5616, or 
MA5620. 
▫
Application scenario: includes FTTB, FTTH, FTTO, and FTTC, represented by B, H, 
O, and C respectively. 

===== PAGE 961 =====
▫
SN:  represented by a 3-digit number. For example, if 2 devices are of the same 
type and have the same physical location, they will be numbered 001 and 002 for 
differentiation. 
•
An example configuration is as follows:
▫
huawei#sysname
▫
{ prompt<S><Length 1-50> }:  PTCMCC-PT-LCBL-MA5680T-01  
▫
PTCMCC-PT-LCBL-MA5680T-01#

===== PAGE 962 =====
•
The naming rules for port description design are as follows:
▫
The description can indicate the ID of the slot to which the local port belongs.
▫
The description can indicate the type of the board where the local port resides.
▫
The description can indicate the ID of the local port. 
▫
The description can indicate the device to which the peer port belongs. 
▫
Other identification information can be added as required.
•
Reference format: slot ID+board type+port ID (to peer NE name)
▫
Board type: represented by the abbreviation of the board, for example, GPBC. 
▫
Peer NE name: represented by physical location+SN. The SN of an NE is a 3-digit 
number. For example, if 2 devices have the same physical location and are of the 
same network level and type, they will be numbered 001 and 002 for 
differentiation. 

===== PAGE 963 =====
•
The naming rules for the line profile description are as follows:
▫
The description can indicate the rate of the line profile.
▫
The description can indicate the interleave mode of the line profile.
▫
The description can indicate the SNR margin of the line profile.
▫
Other identification information can be added as required.
•
Reference format: downstream rate+interleave mode+SNR margin
▫
Downstream rate: represented by numbers, for example, 4, 8, 12, 24.
▫
Interleave mode: represented by the abbreviation of the corresponding mode. For 
example, "I" indicates interleave and "F" indicates fast. 
▫
SNR margin: represented by SNR+value. Generally, the SNR margins include, 3 dB, 
6 dB, 9 dB, 12 dB, and 15 dB. 
•
Example:
▫
12ISNR6 indicates a line profile with the downstream rate of 12 Mbit/s, 
interleave mode of interleave, and SNR margin of 6 dB.

===== PAGE 964 =====
•
The naming rules for the TID description of physical terminals are as follows:
▫
The description can indicate the area of the ONU.
▫
The description can indicate the type of the ONU.
▫
For the ONUs of the same type and the same physical location, different SNs 
need to be used for differentiation.
•
Reference format: area ID+ONU ID+ONU type+/port ID
▫
Area ID: represented by the area code, for example, 10 for Wuxi, 11 Zhenjiang, 
12 Suzhou, 13 Nantong, 14 Yangzhou, 15 Yancheng, 16 Xuzhou, and 17 Huai'an. 
▫
ONU ID: represented by a digit (from 0 to 9), or A to Z if more ONUs are 
involved. ONU IDs are the SNs of ONUs in the same place. 
▫
ONU type: represented by an upper-case letter. For example, "Z" indicates ZTE, C 
Huawei, F FiberHome, and G Wuhan Yangtze Optical Technology Co., Ltd. 
▫
Port ID: represented by a natural number with no limited length. 
•
Example: 
▫
ONU120C/1 indicates the user on port 1 of Huawei ONU 0 in a certain place in 
Suzhou.

===== PAGE 965 =====

===== PAGE 966 =====
•
Reliability design aims to ensure reliable transmission of data on the network, 
including network reliability (such as uplink and downlink) and device reliability (such 
as power supply, control board, other network 1+1 protection isboard, and port). 

===== PAGE 967 =====
•
Currently, network reliability is increasingly valued by carriers. They raise various 
requirements for reliability of access network devices. Single-node reliability 
requirements have upgraded to end-to-end (E2E) reliability requirements. The E2E 
reliability requirements include the following:
•
Device hardware protection requirements:
▫
The control board, power board, and upstream board of the device must support 
the 1+1 redundancy protection.If the active board is faulty, the OLT automatically 
switches services to the standby board to ensure reliable and uninterrupted 
services. Currently, the CO-side devices of various vendors support this protection 
mechanism. 
•
Upstream network protection requirements:
▫
Access network devices are mainly used for access and convergence. If the 
upstream/cascading line is faulty, all the users' services that are converged on the 
device will be interrupted. Therefore, the upstream/cascading line of devices must 
be protected. The options include the protection group and Ethernet link 
aggregation. 
▫
The network 1+1 or 1+N protection is implemented using the path redundancy of 
the network. Currently, the loop network protection solutions STP, RSTP, and 
MSTP are provided for carriers. These solutions use the ring network and certain 
algorithms to implement route redundancy. A ring network is restructured into a 
tree network to avoid proliferation and infinite loops of packets on the ring 
network. If a node is faulty, the system automatically selects another route for 
forwarding traffic. 

===== PAGE 968 =====
•
Redundancy backup of control boards on the OLT: Active and standby control boards 
are configured, which ensures that the services are not affected when the active 
control board is faulty. 
•
OLT power board redundancy protection: Active and standby power boards are 
configured in the system. When the active power board is faulty, the standby power 
board ensures that the device runs properly. 
•
Protection group of upstream ports on the OLT: Active and standby GIU boards are 
configured in the system, and each GIU board provides one upstream port. The two 
upstream ports are aggregated using LACP. If the active upstream port is faulty, traffic 
can be transmitted using the standby upstream port. The protection group of upstream 
ports implements port fault detection, protection switching, and service recovery. 
•
OLT service board redundancy protection: downstream protection is implemented on 
the PON port by installing redundant fibers between the OLT and optical splitter (Type 
B protection). When the active and standby boards are both online, only the optical 
port on the PON port of the active board is enabled. X`

===== PAGE 969 =====
•
A link aggregation group (LAG) aggregates multiple physical links to form a logical 
link for transmitting data at a higher rate. On the Ethernet, one link corresponds to 
one port. Therefore, link aggregation is also called port aggregation. In a sense, an 
aggregation group functions as a port. Link Aggregation Control Protocol (LACP) is the 
control protocol for implementing the link aggregation in the IEEE 802.3ad standard. 
Using this protocol, link layer faults of a port can be detected to control link 
aggregation. 
•
2 to 4 uplinks exist between an OLT and the gateway. Link aggregation ensures 
normal communication between the OLT and the gateway even when certain uplinks 
fail. Link aggregation is the most commonly used upstream protection method used on 
access devices. 
•
Link aggregation supports 3 modes: manual, static, and dynamic. 
▫
Manual link aggregation is used for link protection of devices between which the 
LACP is not supported. The link physical status of a port determines whether the 
port is to be aggregated. Sometimes, the aggregation cannot be controlled 
accurately. 
▫
In static link aggregation mode, devices use LACP for negotiation of port 
aggregation, which feature simple configuration and high reliability. It is the 
most commonly used link aggregation mode. Static link aggregation supports 
the load sharing mode and active/standby mode. When an LAG works in 
active/standby mode, it can also be dual-homed to 2 LSWs. 

===== PAGE 970 =====
▫
Dynamic link aggregation: Aggregation is automatically generated without any 
manual intervention. In practice, however, this type of aggregation is overly 
flexible and is difficult for users to use. (MA5680T/MA5683T does not support 
dynamic link aggregation.)
•
Design suggestions:
▫
It is recommended that inter-board aggregation be used to improve reliability of 
OLTs and upstream devices. 
▫
It is recommended that the OLT and the gateway adopt the LACP protocol to 
prevent faults such as the unidirectional communication. 
▫
It is recommended that the OLT and the gateway support BFD to implement fast 
detection of L3 network fault. This ensures that a fault is detected within 10 ms, 
and the protection switchover is performed within 200 ms.

===== PAGE 971 =====
•
The OLT static LAG is single-homed to an aggregation switch, PTN device, or single-
homed to a BAS in the upstream direction. 
▫
The OLT is configured with GE ports in the upstream direction. The ports are 
aggregated in static LAG mode. In load balancing mode, the upstream 
bandwidth can be expanded and links can be protected. 
▫
The optical uplinks of an OLT are protected. When one optical link is faulty, the 
upstream bandwidth decreases, and the Internet access speed is affected. 
However, services are not interrupted and the customer is unaware of the 
problem.  

===== PAGE 972 =====
•
Protection group is another upstream link aggregation solution. One protection group 
on an upstream port includes one working port and one protection port. In the normal 
state, only the working port carries services. If the link of the working port fails, the 
system automatically switches the service on the working port to the protection port to 
ensure normal service transmission and protect the uplink. 
•
The OLT supports protection groups between different ports on a board and between 
ports on different boards. A protection group has two modes: port state mode and 
time delay mode. 
▫
In port state mode, active ports receive and send signals while standby ports 
receive but do not send signals. The port state mode is originated from the 
networking mode of the interconnection between the IP DSLAM devices and OSN 
devices (as shown in networking mode 1). Other device interconnections are not 
recommended. When the receive direction of an active port is faulty, the active 
port disables its transmit function and notifies the OSN of the link fault. After the 
OSN enables the standby port, the OLT performs protection switching if the OLT 
detects that the port state of the standby control board is linkup. When access 
devices are interconnected with OSN devices, the port state protection group of 
the access devices plays a passive mode. Access devices can implement protection 
switching only after OSN devices complete protection switching. Currently, only 
the GE port on the control board of Huawei MA5600T supports the port state 
mode. Other upstream boards do not support the mode. In addition, the ports on 
OSN devices cannot be configured as an aggregation group. Therefore, pay 
attention to the preceding limitations during planning and design. 

===== PAGE 973 =====
▫
In time delay mode, both the receive and transmit directions of the active port 
are enabled, and those of the standby port are disabled. The time delay 
protection group originates from IP DSLAM and PE dual-homing networking (as 
shown in networking mode 2). The standby port in the time delay protection 
group is shut down. Therefore, the standby link cannot be detected. The standby 
port can be enabled only when the active link is faulty. When the active port fails 
to receive data, the system enables the standby port and switches services to the 
standby link for upstream transmission. The upstream device does not need to 
perform protection switching. According to the switching relationship, the time 
delay protection group plays an active role during protection switching. After 
detecting a fault, the protection group automatically performs switching based 
on the detection result. The upstream boards of an MA5600T support the time 
delay mode. The switching of a physical link takes 1–2s. 
•
Design suggestions:
▫
For dual-homing protection, the time delay protection group mode is 
recommended. The time delay protection group can be configured with ARP 
probe, which can make up the disadvantage that the E2E link status of the port 
cannot be detected based on the port status when there are other cascading 
devices or transmission devices between the OLT and the upper-layer 
convergence device. 

===== PAGE 974 =====
•
A SmartLink protection group is used in dual-upstream connection to provide 
active/standby link redundancy and fast switching. One Smart Link group can contain 
a maximum of two ports, one active port and the other standby port. In normal cases, 
only one port is in active state, and the other is blocked and in standby state. When 
the port in the active state is faulty, the Smart Link group automatically blocks the 
port, and the original blocked port in standby state changes to active state. 
•
Two types of OLT dual-homing protection can be implemented through Smart Link. 
One is that the OLT is dual-homed to 2 aggregation switches, and the switches are 
connected to 2 gateways to form networking. The other is that the OLT is directly 
connected to a gateway using 2 links. 
•
The following describes the scenario where the OLT dual-homing protection is 
implemented using Smart Link:
▫
The LSW or active and standby gateways do not support VRRP+.
•
SmartLink protection mechanism:
▫
The SmartLink is enabled on the OLT to prevent a loop generated on the 
network. When the active link is faulty, the standby port on the OLT is enabled, 
and packets are transmitted upstream using the standby link. The OLT then 
continues its communication with the active gateway through the switch relay. 
▫
Protection switching occurs at L2, and therefore users cannot sense the switching. 
•
Smart Link has a mechanism to update MAC entries and ARP entries for Flush packets, 
and the service switching speed is faster than that of an aggregation group or a 
protection group. Therefore, Smart Link is more suitable for areas with higher 
reliability requirements. SmartLink is a Huawei proprietary protocol that applies only to 
Huawei devices. Based on the limitations of Smart Link and actual application 
experience, aggregation link protection is recommended.

===== PAGE 975 =====
•
The PON protocol document defines 4 downstream protection modes: Type A, Type B, 
Type C, and Type D. 
•
In Type A protection, an extra standby optical fiber is deployed between the OLT and 
the optical splitter, and no automatic protection function is available. In addition, no 
backup function is available for devices. If the backbone fiber is faulty, services need to 
be manually switched to the backup fiber. In this scenario, services are interrupted. The 
interruption time depends on the line recovery time. There is no protection from the 
optical splitter to the user side. If the line to the user is faulty, services are interrupted 
and cannot be backed up. This solution mainly features low costs and is suitable for 
common residential access. 
•
Type B protection aims to protect the reliability of the line between the OLT and the 
optical splitter. 
•
As shown in the preceding figure, the central office has only one OLT and provides 2 
PON ports. The 2 PON ports are connected to the 2:N optical splitter and then to each 
ONU. This solution is backup protection based on OLT ports. The standby OLT PON 
port is in the cold standby state, and the OLT initiates the switchover. The active and 
standby ports can be on the same board or on different boards. The protection applies 
only to the optical fiber fault between the OLT and the ODN and the hardware fault of 
the OLT board. The fault of the line between the optical splitter and the ONU is not 
involved. If the entire system of the OLT is faulty, services of all users are interrupted. 
This solution is cost-effective and applicable to enterprise private-line access. 
•
The dual-homing protection mode was added to the G.984.x series in the ITU Q2 
conference in February 2008. It is a combination of Type B protection and dual-homing 
protection, which indicates that the OLT remote disaster recovery function is added

===== PAGE 976 =====
based on Type B protection. This solution protects backbone optical fibers and OLTs 
in redundancy mode and requires high costs (mainly the costs of OLTs). It is 
applicable 
to scenarios that require OLT remote disaster recovery. 
•
Type C protection aims to protect the line reliability between the OLT and the ONU, 
that is, end-to-end protection on the PON network. 
•
As shown in the preceding figure, the OLT at the CO and the ONU on the user side 
provide 2 PON ports for connecting to 2 optical splitters. Both the OLT and the ONT 
provide 2 GPON ports. The GPON ports of the OLT must work in 1:1 mode. 2 hot 
standby links exist between the OLT and the ONU, and the ONU initiates the switching. 
In active/standby mode, when the active PON port or active optical fiber on the ONU 
fails, the ONU automatically switches the services to the standby PON port. The 
services are then transmitted upstream using the standby optical fiber and standby 
port of the OLT. In this manner, the services are not interrupted. This mode has high 
ODN and ONU costs (ONUs for 2 PON ports are required) and is applicable to 
scenarios where a large number of users are connected to the ONU or most users are 
private-line access users of important enterprises. 
•
The entire OLT system may be faulty. Similar to Type B protection, the ITU also 
proposed the OLT dual-homing mode in February 2008. It is a combination of Type C 
protection and dual-homing protection, which indicates that the OLT remote disaster 
recovery function is added based on Type C protection. This solution has strong 
protection functions, but the OLT and ONU costs are high ((ONUs for 2 PON ports are 
required) and the ODN is complex. Therefore, this solution is applicable to scenarios 
that have requirements on line and OLT remote disaster recovery. （(This solution is 
not recommended currently.) 
•
The end-to-end protection in this scenario is applicable only to the one-level optical 
splitting networking scenario. 

===== PAGE 977 =====

===== PAGE 978 =====
•
In the FTTX solution, user security, system security, and service security are the focus. 
The design content is optional based on the requirements of carriers. 
•
When planning the FTTx network security design, consider information such as the 
networking topology, service type, network construction mode, and customer security 
requirements, and follow the basic rules of high scalability, maintainability, and 
feasibility to avoid complication. 

===== PAGE 979 =====
•
System security issues refer to unstable system performance or configuration errors 
due to an external access (network side or user side) to a system. The possible causes 
and solutions are as follows:
•
DoS attack: A large number of useless packets are sent to a device. As a result, the 
device is overloaded and cannot process or forward other normal packets. 
▫
Solution: The anti-DoS attack is used to resolve the problem. After the anti-DoS 
attack function is enabled, control packets are monitored and those exceeding 
the threshold are dropped. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 
•
ICMP packet attack, that is, an external system sends a large number of ICMP requests 
to a device, and the device is busy processing the requests, causing a sharp decline of 
performance. 
▫
Solution: The anti-ICMP attack function is used. After this function is enabled, a 
device discards the ICMP packets received from the user side whose destination 
IP address is the IP address of the device, protecting the system. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 
•
The permissions of operators are incorrect. Improper operation permissions may cause 
misoperations. 
▫
Solution: Different permissions are allocated for various users, including common 
users, operators, administrators, and super users. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 

===== PAGE 980 =====
•
SNMP security issues:
▫
Solution: SNMPv3 is a user-based security model that provides authentication 
and encryption functions. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 
•
Attack by forging the MAC address of an upper-layer device:
▫
Solution: MAC address filtering is implemented on the source or destination MAC 
address of user packets. Specified well-known MAC addresses and network 
device MAC addresses cannot be filtered. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution when some users forge 
the MAC address of an upper-layer device to attack the system and network. 
•
Customers need to filter out insecure data packets. 
▫
Solution: The ACL is used to filter the packets and streams that match the ACL. 
▫
Design suggestions: Setting the ACL may affect the system performance. 
Therefore, you are advised to use this solution only when the attack is severe. 

===== PAGE 981 =====
•
User security issues can be designed from 6 aspects. 
•
Attacks between users on the same device
▫
Solution: Use Layer 2 isolation and controlled mutual access. That is, a single user 
and a single VLAN can be used to isolate users. User isolation can be 
implemented based on smart VLANs, VLAN stacking, or QinQ. 
▫
Design suggestions: For the Internet access service, QinQ is recommended. For 
details, see VLAN planning. 
•
IP spoofing of DHCP Internet access
▫
Solution: Enable DHCP option 82 on the device, which offsets traditional DHCP's 
defect of no authentication or other security mechanisms. 
▫
Design suggestions: If the Internet access service uses DHCP to obtain IP 
addresses, you are advised to enable DHCP option 82. 
•
Attackers forge authorized users' MAC addresses to launch attacks. 
▫
Solution: Use the anti-MAC spoofing function. Anti-MAC spoofing is used to 
prevent unauthorized users from forging the MAC address of an authorized user 
and sending PPPoE and DHCP control packets and ensure the device security. 
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 
•
Attackers forge IP addresses to attack the system. 
▫
Solution: Anti-IP spoofing is used to guard against attacks from malicious users 
forging IP addresses. 

===== PAGE 982 =====
▫
Design suggestions: You are advised to use this solution during deployment. 
•
User accounts are maliciously used. 
▫
Solution: After the PITP function is enabled globally and on ports, devices can 
work with the RADIUS or BRAS to bind the user account to the access port. In 
this way, the user account can be protected from theft and roaming. 
▫
Design suggestions: If actual conditions permit, PPPoE+ can be selected. 
•
Access of unauthorized ONUs
▫
Solution: Use the ONU serial number, password, or serial number+password
authentication mode. 
▫
Design suggestions: For common users, use the serial number for authentication.

===== PAGE 983 =====
•
Service security design refers to the implementation of different security policies or 
settings based on different services to ensure the normal use of various services. The 
design can be based on 4 types of services. 
•
Attacks on trusted services (such as MDU voice and NMS). Trusted services refer to the 
services that are hard for common users to attack, for example, voice service 
implemented by the built-in IAD of the MDU, and inband network management 
services. In general, trusted services do not have spoofing issues. 
▫
Solution: On a PON network, only VLANs are used to isolate Layer 2 services 
from other services. 
▫
Design suggestions: During deployment, single-tagged VLANs are used. For 
details, see VLAN planning. 
•
For video conferences, SIP phones, and IP PBX services, such services usually obtain IP 
addresses in DHCP mode. The possible causes for attacks on such services include 
MAC/IP spoofing or traffic flooding. 
▫
Solution: Enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing function on the MxU, perform 
dynamic MAC address binding using DHCP, and perform dynamic IP address 
binding on the DHCP server. For heavy traffic, traffic control and VLAN isolation 
can be used to isolate other service traffic. 
▫
Design suggestions: During deployment, enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing 
function on the MxU, and enable service flow control and DBA configuration. For 
details about VLAN service isolation, see VLAN planning. 

===== PAGE 984 =====
•
For IP/MAC spoofing or other attacks on broadband access of small enterprise users 
(IPoE), such services usually obtain IP addresses in DHCP mode. The possible causes for 
attacks on such services include MAC/IP spoofing. The Internet access service may 
encounter other malicious attacks. 
▫
Solution: Enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing function on the MxU, perform 
dynamic MAC address binding using DHCP, and perform dynamic IP address 
binding on the DHCP server. Each small enterprise user can use traffic control 
and S+C-VLAN to isolate other service traffic. 
▫
Design suggestions: During deployment, enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing 
function on the MxU, and enable service flow control and DBA configuration. For 
details about the S+C-VLAN planning, see the VLAN planning. 
•
For IP/MAC spoofing, account theft, or other attacks on common residential users, 
such services usually obtain IP addresses in PPPoE mode. The possible causes for 
attacks on such services include MAC/IP spoofing and account theft. The Internet 
access service may encounter other malicious attacks. 
▫
Solution: Enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing function on the MxU and perform 
dynamic MAC/IP binding using PPPoE. PPPoE+ (PITP) is enabled on the access 
network and the BRAS implements user authentication based on user account 
binding. Each user can be isolated or identified by S+C-VLAN. 
▫
Design suggestions: During deployment, enable the anti-MAC/IP spoofing 
function on the MxU, and enable service flow control and DBA configuration. For 
details about the S+C-VLAN planning, see the VLAN planning. 

===== PAGE 985 =====

===== PAGE 986 =====
•
QoS provides a consistent and expectable data transmission service for users. QoS
involves bandwidth, delay, jitter, packet loss ratio, and availability. QoS technologies 
include: traffic classification and marking, traffic policing and shaping, queue 
scheduling, and congestion management. QoS models are classified into 3 types: best-
Effort service, integrated service (Intserv for short), and differentiated service (DiffServ
for short). 
•
During QoS design, information such as the network topology, service bearing, and 
customer QoS requirements should be designed based on the following rules: high 
scalability, maintainability, feasibility, security, and reliability. ，，，
•
QoS design is to plan service priorities, classify traffic, and mark congestion. 

===== PAGE 987 =====
•
In the Diff-Serv model, the application does not need to apply for resources from the 
network before sending packets. Instead, the application sets the QoS parameters in 
the IP packet header to inform the network node of its QoS requirements. Each router 
on the packet transmission path can obtain the service requirement type of the packet 
by analyzing the IP header.
•
When implementing DiffServ, the access router needs to classify packets and mark the 
service class in the IP packet header. The downstream router only needs to identify 
these service classes and forward the packets. Therefore, DiffServ is a QoS solution 
based on packet flows.
•
Currently, the Diff-serv model is used in the QoS solution of the entire network. That is, 
the QoS parameter information carried in the packet header is used to ensure the QoS 
requirements. Different QoS policies are used for different services on an FTTx network.
•
For the common broadband Internet access service, the best-effort forwarding is 
adopted and the maximum bandwidth of each broadband user is limited.
▫
For VoIP voice, IPTV, and enterprise private line services, bandwidth can be 
guaranteed through bandwidth planning, bandwidth management, and priority 
marking.
•
The QoS solution of the FTTx network uses the Diff-serv model. The CoS parameter 
information carried in the VLAN header of packets ensures the QoS requirements. 
Basic processing flow: traffic classification/priority marking - traffic policing/re-marking 
- queue scheduling/congestion management

===== PAGE 988 =====
•
As the end of the E2E bearer network, an FTTx network plays an important role in 
service access and convergence. QoS on an FTTx network uses the Diffserv model. The 
CoS parameter information carried in the packet VLAN header ensures the QoS 
requirements. The QoS principle for devices is as follows: Network-side packets but not 
user-side packets are reliable. That is, for the PON network, the upstream traffic is 
unreliable and the downstream traffic is reliable. 
•
The basic processing procedure of the QoS for the traffic passing through a PON 
network is as follows: Perform traffic classification and priority marking based on 
related policies, perform traffic policing or re-marking based on related policies, 
perform queue scheduling or congestion management based on related policies, and 
then send the packets out. ，，The related policies refer to some rules or methods and 
are collectively referred to as QoS policies. ，
•
The traffic classification policies refer to the methods of classifying service traffic. 
Generally, traffic classification is performed based on the VLANF. （）：Therefore, plan 
different service VLANs for various service traffic. For details, see VLAN planning. ，，
•
Traffic marking policies: 
▫
In the upstream direction, the traffic-based priority re-marking, the traffic-based 
CAR, and the queuing policy and queue scheduling based on the re-marked 
802.1p priority are adopted. 
▫
In the downstream direction, the traffic-based priority copying, the traffic-based 
CAR, and the queuing policy and queue scheduling based on the 802.1p priority 
are adopted. CAR is determined according to the possible maximum traffic of the 
service, and the queue scheduling policy is PQ. 

===== PAGE 989 =====
•
Policies of traffic policing: 
▫
On a PON device, perform CAR (optional) by using the traffic profile or port. 
Different from other devices, a PON device has DBA rate limitation in the 
upstream direction of the ONU. 
▫
If an upper-layer device supports rate limitation, you are advised to limit the rate 
on the upper-layer device to simplify the configuration. 
•
Congestion control policies:
▫
PQ scheduling is used for high-priority services, 
▫
and WRR scheduling is used for low-priority services. 
•
Upstream DBA policies of an ONU: In an EPON system, DBA rate limitation is 
performed on the ONU. Each ONU is configured with one DBA profile of type 3 
(assured/non-assured bandwidth). In this way, services with a higher priority can be 
guaranteed and bandwidth resources can be shared. In a GPON system, DBA rate 
limitation is performed according to the service type. Different levels of T-CONTs are 
configured based on different service types. For example, requirements of the TDM 
service are high, so the T-CONT type for the TDM service is configured to type 1 (fixed 
bandwidth).

===== PAGE 990 =====
•
When different services are forwarded on the OLT, they can be re-marked with
different 802.1P priorities. The 802.1p priorities range from 0 to 7. Priority 7 is the 
highest priority and is reserved by the system. The priority of the management service 
can be set to 6, which ensures that the device is reachable to the NMS no matter how 
the network is congested. If the carrier requires that the signaling and media are 
separated, you can set the priority of the voice media service greater than or equal to 
that of the voice signaling service. For example, set the priority of the voice media 
service to 5, and voice signaling to 4. The Internet access services have lower priorities. 
If a carrier further classifies Internet access services into VPN, DIA, and common 
Internet access services, the priorities of the services can be set to 2, 1, and 0 
respectively. Plan the priorities based on carriers' requirements.On an OLT, the 
scheduling policy is as follows: Use SP for high-priority services and WRR for low-
priority services. 

===== PAGE 991 =====
•
Traffic policing and DBA policies on a GPON network need to be planned based on 
service types. For details, see the preceding table.
•
The general principle is that the upstream traffic policing or rate limit is performed on 
the ONU, and the downstream traffic policing or rate limitation is performed on the 
OLT or an upper-layer device.

===== PAGE 992 =====
•
The preceding figure shows an example of the QoS process in the GPON upstream 
direction. 
•
In the upstream direction, the user side sends data frames of different services (such as 
HIS, IPTV, and VoIP). After reaching the ONU, the ONU first adds user VLANs to 
different service streams and therefore identifies different services. Different service 
streams are bound to different T-CONTs and different DBA upstream policies can be 
configured for the T-CONTs for traffic policing or rate limitation. After service streams 
reach an OLT, 802.1p priorities can be re-marked on the OLT for the services. For 
example, the priority of HIS can be set to 0, IPTV to 3, and VoIP to 5. Then the service 
streams are forwarded in the upstream direction. An OLT usually has multiple queues, 
and service streams can enter different queues based on 802.1p priorities. The traffic 
that is scheduled by different queues can be sent using the PQ (sequentially sent 
strictly by priority) or WRR policy (sent by queue weight). 

===== PAGE 993 =====
•
The preceding figure shows an example of the QoS process in the GPON downstream 
direction. 
•
After being received from an OLT, the downstream service data is switched to the 
user-side VLAN based on service stream VLANs. The priorities sent from the network 
side are inherited and the downstream traffic rate is limited. Then the service data is 
sent to an ONU. No rate limitation is performed for the downstream service traffic on 
the ONU. 

===== PAGE 994 =====

===== PAGE 995 =====
•
The service access design is to plan the existing services of the carrier and the services 
that may be carried out in the future. The design is mainly to plan service forwarding 
paths and related interconnection parameters. Generally, you need to know the 
networking of the entire network and understand the end-to-end service process. 
•
Broadband service access design: Design the parameters related to broadband access 
based on the protocols PPPoE/IPoE and PPPOA/IPOA. 
•
Voice service access design: Based on the protocol H.248/MGCP/SIP, design the 
parameters related to voice access. 
•
Multicast service access design: Design the parameters related to the multicast access 
service. 
•
Mobile bearer access design: Design the access mode, interconnected devices, and 
interconnected ports for mobile bearer access. 

===== PAGE 996 =====
•
Pay attention to the following factors during broadband service access design:
▫
How does the access network carry and forward data? Is it a single VLAN or a 
QinQ VLAN? Where is QinQ added and where is it terminated? 
▫
How does the metro network forward data? What is the networking structure of 
the metro network? 
▫
BRAS location: Some customers need to manage the BRAS in a centralized 
manner. Generally, the BRAS is located on the upper layer network and needs to 
be authenticated after passing through the metro network. Some customers 
directly place the BRAS on the edge of the metro network, and then the BRAS 
directly accesses the metro network after being authenticated. 
▫
Whether NAT is required and whether the user's Internet access IP address is a 
private IP address. If yes, determine where to perform NAT, on the BRAS or in 
other places. 
▫
Whether the traffic of residential users is the same as that of non-residential 
users, and how to obtain IP addresses,  using PPPoE, DHCP, or static IP. 
▫
Whether to enable the PITP function on the device and where the PITP function 
is enabled.

===== PAGE 997 =====
•
Pay attention to the following factors during the voice service access design:
▫
How does the access network carry and forward data? Where is the VLAN 
terminated?
▫
How does the metro network forward data? What is the networking structure of 
the metro network? Does the voice service pass through the VPN?
▫
Which signaling protocol is used, for example, SIP or H.248. Which authentication 
mode does the protocol use, by domain name, user name, and password. 
▫
Whether media streams pass through the SBC or are directly forwarded on the 
metro network.
▫
Whether the IAD is trusted. Whether the IAD is a built-in IAD or an IAD or PC 
that can be connected to the user side (for example, eSpace). 
▫
Parameters for interconnection between the IAD and NGN. Whether the user side 
has only the voice service but not the fax or modem service. 

===== PAGE 998 =====
•
This slide describes the parameter planning of voice service interconnection of some 
MDUs under an OLT. 

===== PAGE 999 =====
•
During video service access design, pay attention to the following factors:
▫
How does the access network carry and forward data? Where is the VLAN 
terminated?
▫
How does the metro network forward data? What is the networking structure of 
the metro network? Is there a multicast VPN?
▫
Which signaling protocol is used, for example, SIP or H.248. Which authentication 
mode is used by the protocol, by domain name, user name, and password. 
▫
Whether a video stream is unicast or multicast. In the case of multicast, where is 
the replication point?
▫
How does an STB obtain an IP address, using PPPoE or DHCP? 
▫
Whether the multicast VLAN is the same as the unicast VLAN. Whether the OLT 
forwards packets across VLANs. 

===== PAGE 1000 =====
•
Which of the following aspects should be considered during FTTx network reliability 
design?
▫
A: The reliability design of the FTTx network should consider the following three 
aspects: The reliability of the device, reliability on the upstream network side, and 
reliability on the downstream user side are described as follows:
•
How to plan the service priority if the service flow priority on the OLT needs to be 
differentiated?
▫
A: Management service stream > Voice media stream ≥ Voice signaling stream > 
Video stream > Private line Internet access > Common Internet access
•
Which of the following QoS policies are commonly used in FTTx networking?
▫
Answer: Traffic classification, traffic marking, traffic policing, and traffic shaping.
